This appears to be a yeast alpha-glucosidase (likely from Saccharomyces cerevisiae). The sequence contains characteristic motifs of the glycoside hydrolase family 31 (GH31), which includes alpha-glucosidases. Key features I can identify:

This is most likely the yeast alpha-glucosidase (Mal12 gene product from Saccharomyces cerevisiae).

Yeast alpha-glucosidase (Mal12/Mal31/Mal6) is well-studied in enzymology. The literature shows that:
- The optimal pH for maltose hydrolysis is around 5.5
- The optimal pH for glycogen (glucogen) hydrolysis is around 4.5

This makes biological sense because:
- In the acidic vacuole of yeast, alpha-glucosidase functions to break down glycogen/glucomannan storage polysaccharides at lower pH (~4.5)
- In the cytoplasm/periplasmic space where it may act on maltose, the pH is closer to neutral (~5.5)

The substrate-specific pH optima differ because:
- Maltose is a disaccharide with different binding interactions than the larger branched glycogen polymer
- The ionizable groups in the active site that catalyze hydrolysis have different pKa values when interacting with different substrates
- The transition state stabilization differs between substrates

So the answer is A: 5.5 for maltose hydrolysis and 4.5 for glucogen hydrolysis.

This is a well-known finding from studies on yeast alpha-glucosidase, particularly from work by D&#x27;Ennequin, Manners, and others who characterized the enzyme from Saccharomyces cerevisiae.

The key question is: is this a retaining or inverting enzyme?

For β-galactosidases (GH family 2), they are RETAINING glycosidases. The mechanism involves a double-displacement with a covalent glycosyl-enzyme intermediate.

In retaining glycosidases:
- Nucleophile attacks from below the sugar ring (same side as leaving group)
- Acid/base first protonates the glycosidic oxygen (acts as acid), then later acts as base to activate water for hydrolysis

In inverting glycosidases:
- Nucleophile attacks from above (opposite side to leaving group)
- Acid/base only acts as acid (protonates the leaving group)

Some GH47 α-galactosidases from fungi are about 400-450 residues long. For example, the α-galactosidase from Aspergillus niger is about 450 residues.

Given that the question specifically asks about Asp185 and Asp240 as catalytic residues, and given that in GH47 α-galactosidases:
- The nucleophile is the Asp that attacks the anomeric carbon
- The acid/base is the Asp that protonates the glycosidic oxygen

In the mechanism of retaining glycosidases (like GH47):
1. The nucleophile Asp attacks the anomeric carbon (SN2-like), forming a covalent glycosyl-enzyme intermediate
2. The acid/base Asp protonates the leaving group (glycosidic oxygen) during glycosylation
3. In deglycosylation, the same Asp acts as a base to activate a water molecule

The nucleophile Asp is typically positioned below the anomeric carbon and attacks from the α-face (for α-galactosidases that are retaining, they attack from the β-face to produce α-linked intermediate).

In GH47 α-galactosidases based on structural studies:
- The nucleophile Asp is typically located in a &quot;D&quot; that&#x27;s in a β-strand before the main catalytic domain, spatially close to the WAGRMGNS motif
- The acid/base Asp is typically the one in or near the &quot;GDI&quot; motif following WAGRMGNS

Given the choices, the standard assignment in GH47 α-galactosidases is:
- Nucleophile: the &quot;earlier&quot; Asp (more toward the N-terminus in sequence, but spatially positioned below the active site)
- Acid/base: the &quot;later&quot; Asp (the one in or near GDI motif)

If Asp185 is the &quot;earlier&quot; Asp and Asp240 is the &quot;later&quot; Asp (closer to the WAGRMGNS and GDI motifs), then:
- Asp185 = nucleophile
- Asp240 = acid/base (proton donor)

This would correspond to answer C: &quot;Asp185 as the nucleophile; Asp240 as the proton donor.&quot;

This makes biochemical sense because:
1. The nucleophile is typically more buried and positioned directly below the anomeric carbon for nucleophilic attack
2. The acid/base is typically positioned near the glycosidic oxygen to protonate it

In GH47 α-galactosidases (and retaining glycosidases in general), the nucleophile Asp is the one that&#x27;s positioned below the sugar and the acid/base is positioned above, near the glycosidic bond. The nucleophile is typically the &quot;first&quot; catalytic Asp encountered in the sequence (closer to the N-terminus), and the acid/base is the &quot;second&quot; one.

So the answer should be C: Asp185 as the nucleophile; Asp240 as the proton donor.

Let me also consider option A: Asp240 as the nucleophile; Asp185 as the proton donor. This would be unusual for GH47 α-galactosidases, as the spatial arrangement typically places the earlier Asp as the nucleophile.

The answer is C.
